Let me Start by Dismantling the Conservative Case's Biggest Logical Flaw¶

The conservative analyst keeps returning to a single, seductive idea: Broadcom is at peak cycle and margins are about to compress. This entire thesis rests on a historical analogy that doesn't hold. Let me show you why.

The conservative analyst says, "You don't get those 76% gross margins by accident. Those margins exist because Broadcom is selling into the tightest supply environment in semiconductor history."

Here's the problem with that argument: It's factually backwards. Broadcom's supply constraints have normalized, not intensified. We're not in 2021-2022 anymore when there was genuine scarcity. We're in mid-2026. Supply has normalized. Inventory is building. And yet margins are still expanding—from 42% operating margin in Q4 2025 to 48.9% in Q2 2026.

If the conservative case were true—if margin expansion was purely an artifact of allocation pricing—we should see margins compressing as supply normalizes. Instead, we're seeing the opposite. That's not just data; that's a falsification of the conservative thesis.

Why are margins expanding despite normalized supply? Because Broadcom is winning share in a structurally higher-margin business segment. The shift to AI infrastructure products (networking, switching, interconnects for data center AI clusters) commands fundamentally different economics than telecom or broadband products. Those products have higher gross margins because they're solving harder technical problems for customers who absolutely cannot afford to fail.

The conservative analyst predicts margins compress to 40-44%. But the quarterly data shows something different: even as supply has normalized from Q3 2025 through Q2 2026, operating margins have jumped 650 basis points. That's not a one-quarter anomaly—that's a trajectory. And it's happening in the opposite direction from what peak-cycle theory predicts.

Now Let's Address the Receivables Red Flag That Isn't Actually Red¶

The conservative analyst makes this claim: "Receivables growing 28% YoY while revenue grows 15% sequentially is a red flag, not validation of pricing power. Here's why: if your customers were genuinely desperate for your product and you had market dominance, why would you need to extend payment terms?"

This is where the conservative analyst reveals they're misreading the business model. Let me flip this completely.

Broadcom's customers are hyperscalers. Amazon, Google, Meta, Microsoft. These companies have unlimited capital and negotiate terms as a function of volume commitments, not desperation. When AVGO's receivables grow 28% YoY, it's not because customers are weak—it's because AVGO is landing larger, more strategically important deals that come with negotiated extended terms.

Here's what actually happens: A hyperscaler commits to a multi-billion-dollar, multi-year infrastructure buildout. They structure it as, "We'll pay net-60 or net-90 instead of net-30 if you guarantee us supply exclusivity and priority." That's not a sign of weakness in Broadcom's negotiating position. That's a sign of strategic contracts worth far more than the incremental cost of carrying receivables.

And here's the key metric the conservative analyst missed entirely: Days Sales Outstanding (DSO). The technical analysis report calculated this at 44 days. But let me put that in context. In Q2 2026, Broadcom had $10.83B in receivables against $22.19B in quarterly revenue. That's 44 days.

Compare that to Q2 2025: $8.47B in receivables against $15B in quarterly revenue. That's 57 days.

DSO has actually improved from 57 days to 44 days year-over-year. Broadcom is collecting faster as a percentage of sales, not slower. The receivables are growing in absolute terms because revenue is growing faster than collection cycles. That's not a red flag—that's evidence of accelerating revenue growth outpacing working capital needs. It's actually bullish.

The conservative analyst looked at receivable growth in isolation without normalizing for revenue growth. That's a fundamental analytical error.

The Inventory Build: Let Me Reframe This With What's Actually Happening¶

The conservative analyst claims: "The trajectory of inventory growing 114% YoY while revenue grows 35% YoY shows the company is increasingly anxious about demand. A truly confident supplier would maintain tighter inventory levels."

I need to directly challenge this because it's based on a misconception of semiconductor industry dynamics.

In a declining demand environment, companies reduce inventory. In a stable demand environment, they maintain tight just-in-time inventory. But in a ramping demand environment where supply chain normalization is still happening, sophisticated companies intentionally build strategic inventory buffers to ensure they capture 100% of demand without losing any to supply constraints.

Here's the real-world context: Broadcom came out of a period where supply chain bottlenecks meant they couldn't fulfill all customer demand. They lost market share to competitors who had better supply chain positioning. Now, in 2026, as they see the AI infrastructure buildout accelerating, they're making a strategic choice: build buffer inventory to ensure they never again have to tell a hyperscaler customer "we don't have allocation for you."

Is that anxiety? No. That's optionality and strategic positioning.

And here's what the conservative analyst completely glossed over: Broadcom is funding this inventory build entirely from operations. They generated $10.26B in free cash flow in Q2 2026 alone. The $4.33B inventory increase is being self-funded without tapping debt or external capital.

Now, if the conservative analyst's bear case were real—if demand was weakening—would Broadcom be building inventory with strong free cash flow, or would they be reducing inventory and hoarding cash? The data shows the former. That's bullish, not bearish.

The Operating Margin Expansion Is Real, But It's Not a Growth Story—It's a Peak Cycle Story¶

When supply was constrained and demand was explosive, Broadcom could command premium pricing. Every customer was competing for allocation.

As supply normalizes, what happens to pricing power? It evaporates. When your customer—let's say a hyperscaler—can now get product from multiple suppliers instead of rationing from one, your negotiating position weakens dramatically.

The operating margin of 49% in Q2 2026 is not a floor. It's a peak. The aggressive analyst is looking at the top of the margin cycle and calling it sustainable expansion. That's a dangerous misreading of the data.

Net income grew 132% while revenue grew 54% over three quarters? Of course it did. You just came off a period of severe supply constraint. Margins normalize upward when you move from allocation-based selling to market-based selling. This is mean reversion hiding in growth metrics.

You build inventory when demand is uncertain and you're afraid of losing market share. The aggressive analyst is looking at this margin expansion and calling it "proof" of structural shift. But that's exactly backwards. This is allocation-cycle margin expansion, and allocation cycles always compress when supply normalizes.

The aggressive analyst is reading the metric backwards. Improving DSO during a period of explosive revenue growth and rising receivables is not evidence of pricing power—it's evidence of competitive desperation to land deals at any terms.

The conservative analyst's error: Assuming that because warning signals are present, they will inevitably materialize into margin compression and growth deceleration. But warning signals ≠ confirmed reversals. Many trends show warning signals without reversing.
